                                       October 10th, 1963

                               ARE SPACEMEN LD{ING AMONG us777

            Last April 10th, your Editor received a phone call fr01111 a man whc, gave
his name only as Alexander. He was most anxious to meet us; so that evening my wife
Sandy and I went to the Dixie Hotel in New York City, where Alexander was eating din­
ner with two companions who gave their names as Margot Dunmier and Robert Baldwin.
Neither of the two would talk much about themselves, and the woman in particular was
a rather unusual person, dressed in old-fashioned clothes.
            Alexander was a middle-aged man of fairly heavy build and medium height.
He had a dark olive complexion. The only strange thing about him was his foreign ac­
cent, which was impossible to place. He refused to state where he was born - saying
only that it was a country that my wife and I had never heard of. He never quite said
that he was from another planet, but he did claim to have traveled all over the world
without a passport, to have met everyone in saucer research, and to have read absolute­
ly everything available on the subject. He was also interested in psychic phenomena,
and claimed to have extrasensory powers, - although he refused to demonstrate them.
            Alexander's message to us was that he had unlimited funds from some unknown
source, and wanted to finance your Editor on a series of saucer lectures around the
world. The following evening I met him again, but he never got down to details as to
when these lectures would take place, and my original skepticism about the offer was
thereby increased. This time he was eating dinner with a rather attractive young
woman whom he introduced as his secretary. He told me that he had to leave the next
day for a very brief trip to Washington, D.c., and that he would c0ntact me again :imme­
diately upon his return to New York. But that was the last time I saw or heard from
Alexanderl
            Alexander met at least two other saucer researchers while in New York, and
they were as mystified as I was as to his source of funds and his country of origin.
Also unexplained was the motive for his travels. I more or less forgot about the whole
incident until June 6th, when I happened to attend a theatrical party in New York -
completely unconnected with saucers - and ran into the woman whom Alexander had intro­
duced as his secretary-I I had a long talk with her, and learned that contrary to what
he had led me to believe, she had met him only a day or two before I did. He had prom­
ised her employment, had her bonded at his own expense, applied for a passport for her,
showed her an office in New York which he had supposedly rented, - and then left for
Washington. She never heard from him again. Unfortunately, I failed to get her name
or address.
                                         (See Reverse)
              The next phase of this strange adventure occurred in July, when I received
  a series of letters from a well-lmown saucer researcher in St. Louis, Missouri, named
  George Marlo. It developed that a man of the same description, and calling himself
  Alexander, bad visited a number of UFO enthusiasts in St. Louis, including Marlo. He
  then had disappeared from the city as quickly as he had left New Yorkl Marlo was con­
  vinced that Alexander was really a person from another planet, and one of his letters
  to me stated& "I am finished with radio and T.V. appearances about UFO•s. I will talk
  on other subjects, but this one is too dangerous for me. Since talking to Alexander,
  I kwm better AQX .... I won't give out any information to the public that could and
  would cause panic. I can't give you any further details at this time, but may someday.
  Alexander wanted me to be a leader when they land the circular fiying ma.chines •here on
  Earth. They have other means of coming here too, or so he stated •••• Part of his plan
  was to confuse you •••• I found myself talking tQ .:tJ.f1 � three times Dm la Bl xUI.
  U• He would disappear into thin air on certain occasions when other people were
  around." George Marlo went on to explain that he believes Alexander to be indirectly
  responsible for the crash of a police car and the later death of two policemen in St.
  Louis. His fear of Alexander is caused by the fact that he believes him to be a demon,
  or one of the evil space people.
              While talking to me in New York, Alexander mentioned that he expected to
  go to Miami shortly. Although I have no information about his being seen in Miami,
  a weird incident which occurred to saucer researcher William Dunn, Jr., of that city,
  may well have been caused by the same mysterious traveler I met. Bill Dunn wrote me,
  in a letter dated July 14th& "Did I tell you about a recent file robbery that happened
  earlier this year? Well, my UFO files were broken into - the only damn thing that was
  broken into in the entire house, - and many of my valuable reports were burned right
  in my room, and many of my photos, etc., were stolen! I never found out who the per­
  son was. I had the Air Force come down the next day, but they had little sympathy for
  me because it was UFO material••••"
              If we receive any further information about Alexander, we will publish it
  in the next Newsletter. In the meanwhile, we can only wonder about this strange man
  and his motives. If he were a confidence man, he would have asked some researcher for
  money by now. If he were a spy or a secret agent of some sort, he would probably ask
  more questions. Is he really a spaceman? I certainly doubt it, but I can't be sure!
                        ••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••

                          AN EXCLUSIVE INTERVIEW WITH HOWARD MENGER
            In recent months, there has been much speculation among UFO researchers con­
cerning the current activities of saucer contactee Howard Menger. A rumor was circulated
to the effect that Menger would hold a convention this Fall, similar to the one he spon­
sored on his New Jersey farm in September of 1958.
            On October 14th, your Editor received a phone call from Howard Menger, and two
days later we had a four hour meeting in Nev York City. Accompanying your Editor was
Eugene Steinberg, Advertising Manager of SAUCER NEWS.
            It developed that Mr. Menger had driven to Nev York all the way from an un­
disclosed location in the Pennsylvania mountains, apparently just for this one day and
just for the interview. He explained that he is working on a 4-foot radio-controlled
saucer model, to be powered by a new principle he has invented. The complex mechanism,
which he drew for us, involves 1 free electrons under extreme pressure, used together with
certain rare metals which have a high valence. 1
            Before any public convention is held, there is to be a private demonstration
of this machine, called the X4. Scientists and a high-ranking military observer will be
present, according to Menger, and your Editor will also be allowed to attend. Then, at
some time thereafter, - allowing enough time for a publicity build-up, - a convention
will be held. This apparently could not be before the Spring of 1964, as the model is
not yet operating, and Menger does not know when he will be able to complete it, as he is
still overcoming various technical difficulties. He is also short of funds, having given
up his sign painting business.
            The purpose of the convention will be to prove that 1 there are people like us
on other planets, far more advanced than us, scientifically, spiritually, and biological­
ly." However, Menger no longer talks about his space contacts as related in his well­
known book "From Outer Space to You,n and he seems to feel that he was misinformed at
the time as to the origin of the people he was meeting.
            There is a lot more to the Menger story, and we will bring as much of it to
you as possible in the March 1964 issue of SAUCER NEWS. Menger tells us that all new
information for public release will be sent exclusively to our magazine.

           THE EDITOR AND lilS WIFE WISH TO .ANNOUNCE the arrival of their first
child - Elizabeth Barber Moseley, a 7 pound 5 ounce baby girl, born to Sandra
Moseley in Englewood Hospital on October 6th, 1963.
           The delivery was routine, in spite of a Voodoo doll sent to us some
time ago by saucer researcher Richard Ogden of Seattle, Washington. The pack­
age containing the doll gave George Adamski's name as the sender; and a letter
from Ogden received a few weeks later, explained that it was sent "to curse
our unborn child." However, we never believed that Adam.ski was the responsible
party. Mr. Ogden has finally confessed to this and other hoaxes in a letter to
the Editor printed on Pages 5-6 of this issue.
            A second Voodoo doll, sent more recently by a neurotic subscriber
in Chicago (name known to us and the postal authorities), also failed to have
any adverse effect on the birth of the baby.
             MORE MONTHLY MEETINGS OF OUR DISCUSSION AND LECTURE COMMITTEE:
Since April of 1962, the Discussion and Lecture Committee of SAUCER NEWS has
held open meetings in New York City on the third Friday of every month. We
urge all subscribers in the New York area to send us their name and address
for the special Lecture Committee mailing list, so that they may receive no­
tices a few days before each meeting. Our lectures are currently held at 108
West 56th St. (top floor), New York, N. Y. Starting time is 8:30 p.m.
             In August our guest speaker was Gray Barker, who appeared with
your Editor on the Long John radio program in the wee morning hours of August
16th, and spoke for our group that same evening. Because of the Long John pub­
licity, the hall was packed to overflowing, and people had to be turned away.
             On Friday, September 20th, our guest was Stewart Robb, who is a
noted researcher into Fortean phenomena, and a "regular" on the Long John
Show. A full house was obtained through Long John publicity, plus the fact
that your Editor went on Dr. Eden Gray's radio program the previous Tuesday,
to "plug" the lecture. (Dr. Gray is the proprieter of Inspiration House, a New
Age type bookstore located at 129 West 56th St., and she has a program every
weekday from 12:15 to 1:00 p. m. on FM station WNCN in New York.) Stewart
Robb's topic was the prophecies of Nostradamus, the famous 16th Century seer.
There was general agreement that Mr. Robb's lecture was the best that our Com­
mittee had presented up until that time.
             On Friday, October 18th, Gordon Evans gave a lecture for us enti­
tled "The Case for Mars." More details about this will appear in our March
1964 issue.
             In late August, the Discussion and Lecture Committee was reorgan­
ized in hopes of serving its members better. James W. Moseley became Chairman;
John J. Robinson was made Treasurer and also Chairman of the Subcommittee on
Discussions; Eugene Steinberg was made Assistant Chairman; and Sol Lipsky ac­
cepted the position of Assistant Chairman of the Subcommittee on Discussions.
Mary Robinson remains as Secretary. The success of the September and October
meetings was due to the combined efforts of all the above-mentioned people,
plus Gary Oxton, who also gave valuable assistance.

         THE ELEC'IROMAGNETIC EFFECTS Qr. FLYING SAUCERS - (Part One)
                          - by John J. Robinson -

            The reality of the sighting of any unidentified flying object is
transitory. The object is seen, but it soon disappears from view. Unlike a
single wave on a tranquil pond, the UFO does not leave even the evidence the
wave would, - a high water wet area on the shore.
            Since man is subject to hallucinations as well as other mental ab­
errations, it is very easy to accuse any individual of •seeing things: To off­
set such implications, a researcher needs some kind of ph.Ysical evidence.
              One method of collecting a permanent record of anything visual is
that of taking its photograph. However, this method is somewhat ineffectual
when dealing with flying saucers. Photographs of these objects almost always
lack sufficient clarity, and details seen visually are invariably lacking. In
addition, the numerous photographic hoaxes which have been perpetrated help to
make UFO pictures almost valueless in regard to proving anything.
              Because of this lack of physical evidence, people who believe that
some UF0 1 s are intelligently-guided craft, have been widely denounced as being
subject to hallucination, the will to believe, or just plain idiocy. They are
informed that what they really saw was a natural phenomenon, a weather bal­
loon, or some other mundane thing such as a welder's torch or a floodlight.
              There is, however, a very important side effect which has been
apparent at the time of some UFO sightings. This side effect gives excellent
evidence that what was seen is within the realm of physical reality. The phe­
nomenon to which I refer has been recorded and verified for many sightings
during which it served to prove that some type of power source was also pre­
sent, besides the UFO which was observed. The power source seems to generate a
force which is electromagnetic.
              The creation of a natural electric force requires vast potentials
of friction created by huge wind masses or cloud masses. The charge built up
is then a very strong static electric charge. Electricity in nature never dis­
plays any electromagnetism. The static electrically-charged masses drain them­
selves of their potential by bolts of lightning, and the discharges cause the
thunder we hear. These static discharges are not magnetic in their nature, and
the only natural magnetism is that of the Earth itself, which is not strong
enough to move a mass any bigger than a compass needle.
              The events which I shall summarize in this article would require a
mechanism capable of creating a tremendously strong and widely-extended field
of electromagnetism.. Such a mechanism has never been created by our scien­
tists, to the best of my knowledge. The only natural phenomena which might be
compared with UF0 1 s are the plasmoids ("ball lightning"), and recent research
has revealed that these are composed of what appears to be individually charg­
ed particles (electrons), which are held together or confined within an elec­
trostatic field. Thus the plasmoid itself is not electrically charged. The
plasmoid is, however, created by and affected by ionization, while it is also
diamagnetic. This.means that it does not affect the Earth's magnetic fields;
It is affected .m; them. However, the electromagnetic effect of unidentified
flying objects has sho'Wl'l itself to be strong enough to affect low-powered
electrical systems at great distances, doing so at times in a manner which ap­
pears almost premeditated!
              The most numerous low-powered electrically-controlled objects in
our civilization are the conventional gasoline motors found in all automo­
biles. The heart of these motor oars is located in the 6 to 12 volt storage
battery. The current from this battery is changed to a high voltage, low am­
perage spark, which ignites the gas fumes in the cylinders. It is used as a
direct current to power the electrical lighting of' the ear, and is changed
from a direct current to an alternating one by a vibrator in the car 1 s radio,
8.
which then uses a condenser to create the high voltage direct current neces­
sary for receiving and transmitting the, radio waves.
            There is a verified record of over 57 cases which involve the
stalling of automobile motors due to the proximity of UF0 1 s. I shall not at­
tempt to list all of these cases, but will merely summarize a few of the most
interesting ones. A sighting which not only involves the electromagnetic ef­
fect but also an element of humor will serve as a starter. This event happened
during the Levelland "glowing egg" saucer flap. (See footnote.)
           On the night of October 30th, 1957, Hugh Pul.ju took his lady friend
Shirley Moyer for a car ride. They were driving on a road located ten miles
north of Casper, Wyoming, when they suddenly became aware of a large glowing
egg-shaped object which was lying on the highway, directly in front of them.
The object seemed to have two pointed peaks on it and was as large as a house.
Hugh did not have to stop the car, for it sputtered and stalled. Hugh and
Shirley were both frightened, and did ll.2:!i: wish to investigate. They merely
wanted to leave. The sheriff could take over the job of ascertaining just what
type of weird object was obstructing the public right of way - they were not
going tot Hugh stepped on the starter. The motor turned over a couple of times
and then stalled again. Hugh was· determined to leave, and he managed to turn
the car around by repeatedly starting and stalling. Once the ear was turned,
Hugh continued in fits and starts until finally the motor ran continuously.
Then he stepped on the gas and got away as fast as he could go. The sheriff
was duly notified, but when he got there, the roadway was bare.
            There are fewer cases of headlight failure than of motor stalling,
because not all UF0 1 s are encountered at night. Of the 23 cases of headlight
failure on record, there is one which did not involve complete blackout of the
headlights, and it is particularly interesting. The incident involves a squad
ear with two policemen in it, in the town of Elmwood Park, Illinois. This
sighting poses the question of whether or not the electromagnetic force may be
directed and used for a purpose.

                           1ma LIGHT .Qli A � MYSTERY
                            - by N. s. Stringfellow -
            Those with an insatiable craving for mystery should find a rich
feast in Sandy Moseley's article "The Mystery of the Disappearing Planes: fea­
tured in the June 1963 issue of SAUCER NEl-lS. This story is exceptionally well
done, and excites the imagination to the utmost. Another very interesting
story on this subject may be found in Frank Edwards' "Stranger than Science�
under the subtitle of "Sky Traps."
            The incredible thing about the five Navy bombers' plight is found
in the fear and hysteria in the voice and acts of the flight leader: 11 &ergen­
cy - Not sure of our position - Can't be sure where we are - We don't lmow -
Cannot be sure which way is west - Cannot be sure of any direction - Every­
thing wrong - Strange - Ocean doesn't look as it should." The utter state of
chaos and helplessness is shown by the abrupt turnover of the flight command.
What strange dileDllllA caused these experienced Naval airmen to panic, and what
awesome phenomenon caused the complete disappearance of men and machines? It
is hard to contemplate and accept the mysterious disappearance of five planes
and fourteen able-bodied human beings at one time. Later other planes and peo­
ple disappeared in this same area, thus augmenting the unsolved mystery. Sandy
Moseley's article suggests a hole or trap in the sky, and so does Frank Ed­
wards' story.
            Let's look further into this subject. In the July, 1963 issue of
Fate Magazine there appeared a brief story entitled "Paralyzed by a Magnetic
Cloud." This story apparently originated in Philadelphia, and appeared in the
August 13th, 1904 issue of a Hawaiian newspaper. A British steamship from Rou­
mania, making for Delaware Breakwater, ran into a glowing phosporescent mist
which terrorized the crew, played havoc with the ship's compass, and brought
the vessel to a standstill for half an hour. The vessel looked as if it was on
fire, and the sailors flitted about the deck like glowing phantoms. The cloud
had a strange magnetic effect on the ship. The needle on the compass revolved
with the speed of an electric motor, and the sailors were unable to raise
pieces of steel from the magnetized deck. The hair on the heads of the seamen
stood straight on end because of the magnetic power of the cloud, and probably
partly from fright as well. The captain was unable to calm them. The situation
finally became normal when the cloud moved off to sea.
            What caused this strange cloud? Was it a peculiar natural forma­
tion, or was it artificially produced by some kind of super beings as an ex­
periment on earth people and material? Or was it produced by super beings to
hide some operation or activity secretive and important to them? Could this be
a clue to the chaotic condition among the personnel of the Navy planes off the
coast of Florida and their disappearances?
            In Major Donald Keyhoe 1 s book "Flying Saucers - Top Secret," the
story is told of a Navy transport plane which was approaching Newfoundland
over the western Atlantic one night, when lights were seen beneath the plane
like a village in the ocean. The possibility of land beneath was ruled out, as
was the possibility of ships. As the transport began to circle for better ob­
servation, the strange lights quickly dimmed. Several colored rings appeared
and began to spread out. One of them grew in size. The airmen discovered that
it was a giant disc coming up toward them. They did not know where the other
colored discs went.
            What sort of rendezvous of saucers did this Navy transport dis­
turb? We can't help but wonder why the investigating disc did not destroy the
transport. Was it because the operators of the disc decided that the earth
plane had not really seen anything? Could this be another clue to the fate of
the Navy planes off the coast of Florida?
            Did you ever hear the expression "The earth opened up and swallow-
                                                                            ll.
ed them"? Rather than the earth or sky opening up to swallow the five Navy
planes, perhaps the sea opened up to swallow them - a sea trap instead of a
sky trap. Some people claim to have seen saucers "fall" in the sea and into
lakes. Perhaps they did not fall, but went on a straight course back home. Why
couldn't the saucers come up from the bowels of the Earth through the sea, in­
to our atmosphere and beyond? Surface men can now sul:m.erge ships and fire mis­
siles up through the sea to destroy large areas on land. Did the big Navy
transport see saucers coming up from or going down into the sea? Did the five
Navy planes and others off the coast of Florida run into something like this,
and were deemed to have seen so much that they were sprayed by a paralyzing
mist which caused their utter destruction? And were they perhaps taken by
their captors to laboratories deep down in the Earth beneath the sea?
            Recently when the Navy's deep-diving bathysphere went down a mile
and a half in the Atlantic looking for the ill-fated sub Thresher, they found
a crater two hundred feet wide and forty feet deep with no mounding around
its edge. Could this be a place of entry? One might ask if a hole could be
opened in the bottom of the sea to the regions below. If the super beings
could take a nuclear submarine or five airplanes down through such a hole,
they could properly pressurize them or in some manner could control the sea
water.
            Now, dear reader, perhaps you can appreciate the expression used
at the beginning of this article about Sandy Moseley's story "exciting the
imagination to the utmost"; or is it all imagination? There might be more
truth than we think to Davy Jones• Locker or King Neptune's Realm. In recent
years Earth's scientists have definitely turned to a serious study of the sea.
What has caused this trend in scientific investigation?

                          SPACEMEN Ili .Q!IB MIDST
                            - by Gray Barker -
           Are spacemen really living among us, as many UFO researchers claim?
Personally, I have never seen a spaceman, nor have I ever seen a flying sau­
cer, for that matter: but I like to consider all the many claims and theories
in an effort to arrive at a conclusion.
            Contactees such as George Adamski and Howard Menger have written
that people from other planets visit us regularly, but that only on rare oc­
casions do they make their identities known to earth people. Apparently these
extraterrestrials compare reasonably enough in appearance to earthmen, so that
by donning terrestrial garb and mastering our languages, they can live right
among us without being identified. They even take jobs alongside earthmen, may
occupy governmental positions, and very quietly go about whatever they want to
do here.
            UFO skeptics pose quite a problem to us enthusiasts when they ask
us why don't the saucers land openly and make themselves known if they are
really coming here. Similarly they wonder why space people don't make them­
selves known to us if they are actually living here among us.
            To understand the arguments for the case of space people living
here, we must first review the role of such extraterrestrials as expressed in
UFO literature and thought. We on this planet are in serious danger of blowing
ourselves up in great numbers - possibly to the extent of almost completely
denuding earth of intelligent life. Although some space communications have
hinted that an atomic war might destroy the entire earth and have a harmful
effect upon neighboring planets, the most usual role played by the spacemen is
a sincere desire to help us advance morally and spiritually, with the bomb de­
struction aspect played down.
            Some enthusiasts believe that since we are almost ready to visit
12.

other planets and become a mobile member of the space community, the visitors
are preparing us gradually for this role.
             One writer, Laura Mundo, head of Detroit's Interplanetary Founda­
tion and author of the new book "Flying Saucers and the Father's Plan," has a
more complicated but perhaps a more sensible explanation. She believes that
earth people were reincarnated here from other planets, and even brought here
physically, in saucers, when they failed to progress suitably on Venus and
other locations. In other words, they were demoted to a planet of lower vibra­
tional status, namely earth, where they could have an opportunity to make mor­
al and spiritual progress toward promotion back to their original homes.
             But the earth and the entire solar system is now moving into a
section of the Universe where there is a higher vibrational pattern, and as a
result, our own planet's physical vibration is rapidly increasing. Earthmen,
however, are not matching this increased vibrational status in their spiritual
understanding and development, and will have to be again moved to some other
planet of lower vibration if this vibrational increase is not hurried up. The
visitors, says Laura Mundo, are here in ever-increasing numbers to speed up
the process.
             The casual observer on the Ufological scene would immediately sug­
gest that if the space people are really here, then they should come out into
the open, make themselves known, and speed up progress. After all, there would
no longer be any doubt, and everyone would listen to them and do as they said.
            However, Laura Mundo says that space people of antiquity� open­
ly contact earth people and assist them both morally and technologically.
But earthlings began to worship the visitors as gods, rather than understand
their true part in the "Father's Plan."
            Moral and spiritual progress are made only when there is a will­
ingness to do so: and in general, mankind enjoys being told how to behave, and
would rather blindly follow instructions than to initiate its own judgments.
            If man is to accomplish meaningful progress on his own, assistance
must be minor and consist mainly of directional guidance. This the space peo­
ple are subtly trying to do. Now and then they may openly contact a Menger, an
Adamski, or some other figure who can popularize the incident, in order to
gauge public reaction to a possible appearance. This they can do without real­
ly being detected, because only a handful of the public will actually believe
the contact accounts.
            In her book, Laura Mundo
relates many odd ways in which space
people go about their indirect guid­
ance. A visiting spaceman even tried
to break up a saucer lecture in De­
troit, just to give the people in­
volved a chance to continue under
hardship and have a better chance to
learn. Even though I am still in the
skeptic's camp, explanations such as
those given by Laura Mundo come
nearer to convincing me than the
usual contactee stories.

                     HQ:l ANJMALs .rn rn wrToouT c1ocKs
                          - by Eugene R. Steinberg•
             As described in a recent issue of Life Magazine, most animals have
a mysterious ability to regulate their activities by means of some physiolog­
ical process which enables them to tell time. For example, turtJ.es and pigeons
apparentJ.y get their directional fix by watching the position of the sun or
stars; but, as the sun and stars appear to be moving in the sky as the earth
turns, these animals must have some way of knowing how much the earth has
turned. Humans have chronometers to do this. Animals use mysterious biological
clocks which are almost as accurate!
             The most amazing thing of all is that these animal "clocks" go on
functioning even when the animals are put into laboratories, under conditions
of controlled light and temperature, where they are hidden from all the obvi­
ous clues of the passage of time. For example, a certain breed of crabs habit­
ually hides in marshes near the sea at high tide, and runs out at low tide to
feed on the fiats exposed by the receding waters. Every day, low tide comes 50
minutes later than the day before, and the peak of crab activity is corre­
spondingly later. In the laboratory, these crabs follow exactly the same pat­
tern as if they were still living in their natural habitat. Also, the crabs
living outdoors darken their skin as a protection against sunburn. The crabs
in the laboratory, who see no sun, darken their skins in the same way at the
same time!
             Scientists have found that under constant laboratory conditions,
animal clocks do not run exactJ.y on a 24-hour day, but always very close to
it. In some animals, as many as 20 different processes have been studied, each
with an approximately 24-hour cycle. The observations made have been very pre­
cise, but scientists are unable to agree on the� of these cyclical
rhythms. One theory, advanced by Dr. Frank Brown of Northwestern University,
is that animals depend on some outside source of information such as changes
in barometric pressure, or in the earth's magnetic field, or in the intensity
of cosmic radiation. The alternate theory, advanced by Dr. Colin Pittendrigh
and a team of researchers at Princeton University, is that the clock is an in­
ternal self-contained timer, rather than a forced response to geophysical
forces. In this view, the clock is a property of protoplasm itself. The proto­
plasmic clock evolved with a period close to that of the tides or the sunrise
simply because this was an advantage to the life of the animal.
             Whatever its cause, the importance of this biological clock is in­
disputable. The United States Navy and Air Force, which helped to finance re­
search into the riddle, are interested because they would like to bring the
simplicity and precision of the animal timers to the bulky, complex machines
which guide their ships and planes around the world. Doctors are also inter­
ested, because health is apparently related to the proper functioning of the
internal clocks. It has been found, for instance, that chick embryos survive
more operations at one time of day than another. It has also been learned that
certain toxic drugs help mice when administered at one time of day, and kill
them when administered at another. This indicates that with further research,
Man's health can be improved by a better understanding of the mysterious in­
ternal clock which we too have within our bodies.

                             - by Sandy Moseley -
             It was the week of August 15th, when Gray Barker was to appear on
the Long John Program, be guest speaker at the SAUCER NEWS Discussion and Lec­
ture Committee, and attend our annual saucer party.
             I suggested to Jim. that Gray be our house guest. At first he was
opposed to the idea; but I said that this would give us both an opportunity to
get to know Gray better.
             Gray accepted our invitation and arrived with several suitcases
and a box of books to sell. The first night we listened to a "word jazz 11 re­
cord album he had brought along. These records are very abstract, and although
Jim. enjoyed them, I found them depressing - like Gray's poetry.
             After a few beers, Gray's shyness started to disappear. He kicked
off his white bucks and began exuding his charming personality. He is a typi­
cal bachelor. There is madness to his method - for example, he brought a win­
ter suit to New York in August!
             The next evening Gray and Jim spoke at the saucer meeting. Gray is
a good lecturer, with a pleasant drawl and a good command of the English lan­
guage. As a matter of fact, he once taught English in a high school in West
Virginia and was head of the visual aids department. He worked his way through
college by being a movie projectionist, and is very interested in the theatre.
             We all have our secret ambitions, and Gray's is to re-make the
silent picture classic, "Birth of a Nation." Besides publishing books, Gray is
in the theatre booking business in Clarksburg.
             Despite their disagreements in the saucer field, Gray and Jim. have
a lot in common - terrible taste in art, a love for sick poetry and records,
and a liking for similar books and movies.
             Gray stands 6• 5", has dull blue eyes, brown hair, and is consid­
ered handsome. He is an intellectual, a Christian, and an animal lover. While
here he became attached to our cat Arnold, which he brought back to Clarksburg
with h:iln.
             Gray and I had a wonderful rapport, and even Jiln reluctantly ad­
mits that he was sorry to see Gray leave. Which just goes to show that one can
accept and like others even if their ideals and ideas are not exactly alike.
It took broadmindedness on Jiln and Gray's part to make this visit possible.
And Jim., in all fairness, has consented to publish this article at my request •

        FURTHER INFORMATION ABOUT JONATHAN SWIFT Alli2m  MOONS OF HAM
                                                            l
                  - by Robert J. Durant and James W. Moseley -
            The material in recent issues of SAUCER NE.WS about Jonathan Swift's
moons of Mars prompted us to re-read "Gulliver's Travels" with a view to dig­
ging out other material of interest to our readers. (See Page 14, March SAUCER
NE.WS and Page 6, June S. N.)
             The research was fruitful. It appears that Swift had a great deal
to say on "saucerian" subjects. Below is a summary of pertinent material taken
from Part II, "A Voyage to Leputa, 11 chapters 1, 2, and 3. The moon data is to
be found in the same section of the book, which was written in 1735.
             (Gulliver has been adrift in a small boat. He comes to an island,
catches some sleep, and then goes for a walk.)
             "I walked awhile among the rocks. The sky was perfectly clear, and
the sun was so hot that I was forced to turn my face from it. All of a sudden
it became obscured in a manner very different from what happens by the inter­
position of a cloud. I turned back, and perceived a vast opaque body between
me and the sun, moving forward towards the island. It seemed to be about two
miles high, and hid the sun for six or seven minutes••••As it approached near­
er over the place where I was, it appeared to be of a firm substance, the bot­
tom fiat, smooth, and shining very bright from the reflection of the sea be­
low. I stood upon a height about two hundred yards from the shore, and saw
this vast body descending almost to a parallel with me, at less than an Eng­
lish mile distance. I took my pocket perspective and could plainly discover
numbers of people moving up and down the sides of it••••"
              (The body turns out to be an island in the sky. Gulliver is rescu­
ed by a seat lowered from the island.)
             "His majesty had given orders that the island should move north­
east by east to the vertical point over Lagado, the metropolis of the whole
kingdom below upon the firm earth. It was about ninety leagues distant (270
miles) and our voyage lasted four days and a half. I was not in the least sen­
sible of the progressive motion made in the air by the island." (Thus, the
speed of travel was about 2½ miles per hour.)
             11 ••••
                     The flying or floating island is exactly circular, its diam­
eter 7,837 yards, or about four miles and a half, and consequently contains
ten thousand acres. It is three hundred yards thick. The bottom or under sur­
face, which appears to those who view it from below, is one even regular plate
of adamant shooting up to the height of about two hundred yards. Above it lie
several minerals in their usual order, and over all is a coat of rich mold,
ten or twelve feet deep.
             "At the center of the island there is a chasm about fifty yards in
diameter, from whence the astronomers descend into a large dome, which is
therefore called 1 fiandona gagnole,' or the astronomers' cave, situated at the
depth of a hundred yards beneath the upper surface of the adamant. In this
cave are t:wenty lamps, continually burning, which, from the reflection of the
adamant, cast a strong light into every part. The place is stored with a great
variety of sextants, quadrants, telescopes, astrolabes, and other astronomical
instruments. But the greatest curiosity, upon which the fate of the island de­
pends, is a loadstone of a prodigious size, in shape resembling a weaver's
shuttle. It is in length six yards, and in the thickest part at least three
yards over. This magnet is sustained by a very strong axle of adamant passing
through its middle, upon which it plays, and is poised so exactly that the
weakest hand can turn it. It is hooped around with a hollow cylinder of ada­
mant, four feet deep, as many thick, and twelve yards in diameter, placed hor­
izontally, and supported by eight adamantine feet, each six yards high. In the
middle of the concave side there is a groove twelve inches deep, in which the
extremities of the axle are lodged, and turned round as there is occasion.
             "The stone cannot be moved from its place by any force, because
the hoop and its feet are one continued piece with that body of adamant which
constitutes the bottom of the island.
             "By means of this loadstone, the island is made to rise and fall,
and move from one place to another. For with respect to that part of the earth
over which the monarch presides, the stone is endued at one of its sides with
an attractive power, and at the other with a repulsive power. Upon placing the
magnet erect, with the attracting end towards the earth, the island descends;
but when the repelling extremity points downward, the island mounts directly
upwards. When the position of the stone is oblique, the motion of the island
is so too; for in this magnet the forces always act in lines parallel to its
direction."
             (There follows a step by step explanation of navigation by use of
the loadstone, as given belows)
             "When the stone is put parallel to the plane of the horizon, the
island stands still; for in that case the extremities of it, being of equal
distance from the earth, act with equal force, the one drawing downwards, the
other pushing upwards, and consequently no motion can ensue.
16.
            "The loadstone is under the care and pro­
tection of certain astronomers who, from time to
time, give it such positions as the monarch directs.
They spend the greatest part of their lives observing
the celestial bodies, which they do by the assistance
of glasses far excelling ours in goodness."
             (Here Swift states, in a matter of fact
manner, that the astronomers have discovered two
moons of Mars, and he gives their distances from the
planet and their periods of revolution.)
            "If any town should engage in rebellion
or mutiny, fall into violent factions, or refuse to
pay the usual tribute, the king has two methods of
reducing them to obedience. The first and mildest is,   "But EVERYBODY I
of course, by keeping the island hovering over such a phone keeps saying it's
town and the lands around it, whereby he can deprive     not their department!"
them of the benefit of the sun and rain, and conse­
quently afflict the inhabitants with dearth and diseases; and if the crime de­
serves it, they are at the same time pelted with great stones, against which
they have no defense••••"
            Is there any reason to take seriously this suggestion of an "is­
land in the sky" populated by scientists and powered by an anti-gravity de­
vice? The remarkable accuracy of Swift's statements about the moons of Mars
forces us to take a good long look at the rest of his claims. At least we can
examine them for consistency, and perhaps do some second guessing in terms of
our present-day scientific information.
            The island of Laputa, says Swift, consists of a base rock layer
two hundred yards thick, covered with a one hundred yard layer of topsoil.
This would make the island weigh something like 100 million pounds, assuming a
conservative figure of 5 for the average density of the island. It is simply
not possible for an axle 12 inches in diameter to sustain such a weight, and
that is what Swift seems to think is happening. The loadstone, at best, could
only be a small guiding force working in conjunction with a gravity nullifying
device.
            A close look at the forces involved will explain our point. In or­
der to move the island, three forces must be overcome: The island's weight
must be counterbalanced; the inertia of the island must be overcome to get it
moving, and to stop or change the direction of movement; and finally, wind re­
sistance must be overcome. Even at the leisurely 2½ miles per hour which Swift
experienced on his trip to Lagado, the wind resistance of the island would be
tremendous. An anti-gravity device would, theoretically, take care of the
weight and possibly the inertia of the island, leaving the loadstone to battle
wind resistance alone. But it is inconceivable that the spindly arrangement in
the Astronomer's Cave could manage such forces. Compare the amount of rigging
required on a sailing vessel with the size and mounting of the loadstone.
            The loadstone can't have the force field properties of a magnet
and operate in the manner Swift describes. He says that the stone can be "mov­
ed by the weakest hand," and that it will remain in any position the operator
chooses. ''When the stone is placed parallel to the horizon, the island stands
still; for in that case the extremities of it, being at equal distance from
the earth, act with equal force, one in drawing downward, the other in pushing
upwards, and consequently no motion can ensue." But if one end of the stone
attracted the earth and the other repelled the earth with force enough to move
that great mass, the loadstone would flip to a vertical position, with the at­
tracting side down, and stay there. Nothing could move it. For the loadstone
to function as advertised, it would have to be a ray device, emanating at­
tracting and repelling rays from its ends.
            Incidentally, the words 11 Leputa, 11 "flandona, 11 and "gagnole" were
analyzed with the Shaver Mantong Alphabet. We regret to say that the results
did not throw much light on what Mr. Swift was up to, or what Mr. Shaver was
up to, either.
            Getting back to the moons of Mars, a recent feature article in the
"National Enquirer" claims that U. s. scientists are working on a round-the­
clock investigation of Phobos. Many leading scientists believe that this lit­
tle moon, 5 miles in diameter, is probably a huge space platform, built by
living creatures from Mars or some other planet.
            According to this article, the United States Gove�tllllent is going
to spend over $55 million in the next year or so, in an attempt to find out if
this theory is true. Says Raymond E. Wilson, Chief of Applied Mathematics for
the National Aeronautics and Space Administration: "Space probes are now being
prepared to determine whether Phobos is actually a huge orbiting space base."
            It is also believed that the next Mariner space rocket, scheduled
for launching late this Fall or in the early spring of 1964, will be aimed to
pass close to Mars and will contain special equipment capable of analyzing the
light reflected from Phobos. Scientists say that if this moon reflects light
in the way aluminum does, it will prove Phobos to be a space platform rather
than a natural moon.
            Phobos and its sister moon, Deimos, were discovered by the U.S.
astronomer Asaph Hall in 1877, - though they were described with great accura­
cy in Swift's book over a hundred years earlier. For many years after their
official discovery, nobody paid much attention to them because they are so
small. There haven't even been any good photographs taken of them, because the
Earth's atmosphere interferes with cameras, and thus the reflected light from
Mars tends to blank out Phobos.
            Finally, in 1959, I.S. Shklovsky, who is considered to be Russia's
leading planetary physicist, published the results of observations he had
made. He came to the conclusion that Phobos is an artificial satellite, proba­
bly made of aluminum. He believes that this moon is hollow inside, something
like a tin can from which the contents have been removed. Shklovsky had good
reasons for his theory about Phobos - the most important of which is that the
tiny moon moves in its orbit over three times faster than Mars I This is impos­
sible for a natural moon, according to accepted astronomical theories. For
another thing, Phobos is slowing down as time goes by, and moving closer to
Mars. This duplicates the motions of all man-made satellites put into orbit by
the United States and Russia.
            One of the leading scientists to express agreement with Shklovsky
is Dr. H.M. Sinton, an astronomer at Yerkes Observatory, Williams Bay, Wiscon­
sin. In a statement to the U. s. National Academy of Sciences, Yerkes said:
"Phobos may be a huge orbiting city filled with men, women, and children. The
other moon (Deimos) might be one too. 11 Also in agreement is Dr. Fred Hoyle, a
professor of astronomy at Cambridge University, England. He is quoted as say­
ing that this is the only theory he has ever heard that covers the mystery of
these two moons.
            Disagreement has come from other scientists, including astronomer
Clyde W. Tombaugh, associate professor of earth sciences at the Research Cen­
ter of New Mexico University. Dr. Tombaugh is world-famous as the discoverer
of the planet Pluto, and is known in saucer circles for his sensational close­
up sighting of a saucer with apparent lighted windows, back in 1949. However,
regarding the moons of Mars, he is quoted in the Enquirer as saying: "Putting
up such a satellite would severely strain the capabilities of even a world
rich in resources. The extreme poverty of mineral resources on Ma.rs would have
deprived them of the necessary materials."
            So the controversy goes on; and it will probably be another few
years before it is settled for once and for all.

                             RECENT� STORIES
             MYSTERY CRATER IN ENGLAND: One of the most publicized UFO cases in
recent years occurred last July near the town of Charlton, Wiltshire, England.
A crater about eight feet wide was found in a potato patch belonging to a
farm.er named Roy Blanchard, who saidz 11 As far as I'm concerned, something
strange landed in those fields. And when it took off again, some of my pota­
toes went with it. 11
             Although at least two reliable local people saw something flash
through the sky on the night of July 10th, in the vicinity of the Blanchard
farm., apparently no one actually witnessed the UFO landing (if such it was.)
However, the headlines in the wire service stories about the crater were grab­
bed by one Dr. Robert Randall, who joined British Army investigators at the
scene. He solemnly declared that the hole had been made by a 600-ton space­
ship, .500 feet wide, from the planet Uranus. Dr. Randall calculated that the
saucer was carrying a crew of 60, and that it had made a forced landing in the
field when its solar batteries failed. An Army bomb disposal unit got a read­
ing from the crater on their metal detector, and this indicated to Dr. Randall
that the saucer had broken off one of its three 80-foot-long extensions used
as landing gear. The other two tripod feet left marks in other fields about
500 feet away from the crater, according to Dr. Randall, who made a detailed
drawing of the imaginary saucer for the newspapers.
             The British press went into a furor over the incident, and two
members of the House of Commons asked for a governmental investigation, to de­
termine whether or not an extraterrestrial craft was actually involved. Wave­
ney Girvan, editor of "Flying Saucer Review," also got into the act, but for­
tunately the editor of 11 Uranus 11 (another British saucer magazine) did not.
Meanwhile, attempts to check on the credentials of Dr. Randall, said to be an
Australian astrophysicist, led to the fact that he was not known to The Royal
Society, Britain's top scientific body. It was never determined just what sort
of doctor Dr. Randall is. According to one story, a reporter went to his house
in a nearby town and asked his wife if he was a doctor of medicine or of phys­
ics. She replied, "Both," and slammed the door.
             If it had not been for Dr. Randall's "lunatic fringe" remarks
(i. e., how could he possibly know which planet the alleged spaceship was
from?), this would have been a remarkably sober and interesting UFO case. The
mine detection team, shown in the photo on the cover, roped off the area from
the curiosity-seeking public, and dug six feet down. Within a few days after
the discovery of the mystery crater, the case was "solved" by the finding of a
small rock, assumed to be a meteorite, which was sent to the British Museum
for examination. But alas, to the disappointment of the skeptics, the rock
turned out to be iron pyrites indigenous to the area, and definitely not a
meteor. A later report quoted a British scientist as saying that the hole
might have been caused by a meteorite which fragmented upon hitting the
earth's surface; but basically, the crater remained unexplained. It seems in­
credible that either a meteor ,2.t a 600-ton spaceship could have landed without
making enough noise to wake up the whole countryside!
             Almost overlooked in the controversy over the Charlton incident,
were two even more mysterious craters found at about the same time in a lonely
district of Berwickshire, Scotland. A 60-year-old farm.er named James Brown
discovered these holes, which were sixteen feet wide and three feet deep. The
craters were twelve feet apart, and great lumps of earth from them had been
hurled as far away as forty feet. Strangest of all, channels up to 44 feet
long were found radiating from the craters like the spokes of a wheel. Also, a
group of small square holes one foot wide and two feet deep was discovered
about 35 feet from the craters. Local police admitted they were baffled, and
called in a scientist from Edinburgh Observatory to investigate. Three Army
                                                                                             19.
bomb-disposal experts spent a day digging, found nothing, and gave up.
             Our latest information on this matter is th.at a man named John
Southern claims that the English crater as well as the two in ScotJ.and were
dug by him.self and two friends, as a hoax. However, this explanation seems
� unlikely!
            SEA SERPENT SEEN OFF NEl-1 JERSEY COAST: Last July, a sea monster as
weird as anything Loch Ness has produced, was seen near a scientific research
vessel at Sandy Hook, New Jersey, - just 25 miles from Times Square. The
sighting is important because it was made by several reputable people, includ­
ing Dr. Lionel A. Walford, director of the United States Fish and Wildlife Re­
search Center at Sandy Hook. Said Dr. Walford: "It was an invertibrate. It
looked like so much jelly. I could see no bones, and no eyes, nose, or mouth.
But there it was, undulating along, looking as if it were almost made of fluid
glass. A number of us saw it. It was at least 4-0 feet long and about 5 inches
thick, looking something like an enormously long flattened eel. 11
            The creature also passed within a few feet of one of the vessel's
divers, as he sat in a cage on the ocean bottom. observing marine life. He left
the cage and swam after the sea serpent, but it outdistanced him, and he re­
turned to the research ship before sharks could start investigating �t
            On August 22nd, a man at nearby Belmar, New Jersey, reported see­
ing a giant jelly-like fish about 30 to 4-0 feet in diameter. He was fishing
from a jetty when he spotted the creature, which was apparentJ.y not the same
serpent seen by the scientific team. This one looked like a round cake of ice
but was flexible. It was almost transparent, and moved in ripples. It disap­
peared after about 15 minutes, said the fisherman.
            The scientific research ship returned to the area in an effort to
photograph or capture the sea serpent. Aboard were sixteen scientists and div­
ers, and their ship was accompanied by a chartered press boat carrying newsmen
whose interest had been aroused by the sightings. But nothing further was
seen, and the story ended in the usual babel of controversy as to whether or
not the sightings were misinterpretations of conventional fish.

months occurred last August in various communities in the state of Illinois.
The best sighting was made on the night of August 4th by a teenager named Ron­
nie Austin, of Keens, llinois, who gave the following account:

            "When we reached my girl's house, her sister got up and came out­

speed I ever saw. I can't imagine what it was."
            A team of Air Force investigators eventually came to lliinois to
look into this and dozens of other UFO stories which followed in quick succes­
sion during the next few days. The team, headed by Lt. Col. Robert Friend of
Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, concluded that Ronnie Austin's sighting was
caused by the moon and the planet Jupiter, which was bright and low in the
horizon that night. Such an explanation does not come anywhere close to cover­
ing all the details of young Austin's report, however, - especially the part
about radio interference, which is a common phenomenon in close-up UFO sight­
ings.
            Several of the sightings in this Illinois flap were apparently
caused by misinterpretation of an advertising sign being towed by an airplane.
When seen at an angle, this sign appeared to be a cigar-shaped mystery craft,
and some reports described the light bulbs as lighted windows. However, there
were a few UFO reports from the Chicago area in early August which, although
not as sensational as the one made by Austin, nevertheless seem authentic.
            FORTEAN ITEMS: An 11-pound chunk of ice which fell near Moscow in
early September is believed by Russian scientists to have come from outer
space. Said Tass, the official Russian news agency: "It is absolutely impossi­
ble for the ice to be of atmospheric origin, as clear sunny weather prevailed
on that day. The hypothesis that it fell off a plane flying at great height is
also rejected." The ice will be subjected to a complicated analysis with iso­
topes••••About August 28th, small squares of bright red paper were found scat­
tered over an area of ten square miles west of Larned, Kansas. No explanation
was found••••On August 29th, straw fell for an hour from clouds over Dartford,
Kent, England. Joyce Hyams, wife of a farmer on the outskirts of the communi­
ty, said: "I was hanging out some washing when I felt straw falling all over
me. I looked up and the sky seemed full of it." The strawfall quickly spread
to the city of Dartford, and soon local residents were busy with brooms and
                                                                            21..
pitchforks removing it from streets and lawns. The weird phenomenon stopped as
suddenly as it had begun. Local police were baffled, as there was far too much
of the straw for it to have dropped from an airplane••••
             Millions of tiny frogs poured out of the sky during a rainstorm.
which occurred on July 17th near Silver Lake in southeastern Oregon. Mrs. R.
W. Carpenter of Eugene, Oregon, says that she and her husband were driving
along a highway when the mysterious phenomenon began. Many of the frogs landed
on the highway and were killed by traffic. Others fell in an area estimated by
Mrs. Carpenter to be a half mile in diameter. The frogs were about the size of
a quarter and grayish in color. Perm.anent residents of the Silver Lake area
told her that the species is not native there. (Our thanks to subscriber
Charles Edmonds for this item.) •••••O:n August 12th, the communications satel­
lite called Telstar II returned to life as mysteriously as it had ceased oper­
ation almost a month previously. Bell Telephone Laboratories engineers con­
fessed that they know no more about what caused the satellite to resume func­
tioning than they know about what stopped it in the first place. The discovery
that Telstar II had resumed functioning was made when a routine signal was
sent to it, and to the surprise of the engineers, the satellite answered••••
             Here's a really weird onez On the night of September 4th, several
persons in a Hollywood, California neighborhood claimed they were awakened by
a terrific crash and that they saw an overturned car, badly smashed and burn­
ing on the street. The witnesses said that police and fire units were soon on
the scene, followed by an ambulance which carried away a woman, apparently
seriously injured. But Hollywood police have no record of such an accident,
nor do they have any evidence that police were sent to the supposed accident
site. The fire department likewise claims that they know nothing of the event.
A local hospital confirms that an ambulance was sent to this location and that
a woman was treated for very minor injuries sustained in a traffic accident
there. She was released within fifteen minutes by the hospital. But the site
shows several smashed mailboxes, a brick wall with a large chunk out of it,
and recently-trampled bushes. No one lmows what happened to the car, the po­
licemen, or the firemen that the residents clailll they saw. (Our thanks to re­
searcher Manon Darlaine for this item.) •••••Henry Thorne, one of the trapped
coal miners whose rescue received nation-wide attention last August, reported
that he and the man trapped with him saw some very strange things. These phe­
nomena, assumed by the newspapers to be hallucinations, were seen at the same
time by both of the cave-in victims. Says Thorne: 11 0:n the fifth day I saw all
kinds of lights and the actual figures of people. The lights and figures were
always in front of us, but the more we crawled toward them, the further away
they got. For example, I saw a man, or the dark shape of a man, with a light
on his helmet. I yelled, 1 Show me some light over heret 1 Davy saw him too, but
the shape of the man got smaller as we crawled toward him and then he was gone
altogether."••••
             A recent undated item tells of a frightening hairy monster that
has been seen by many people living in a district near Mansfield, Ohio. A
hairy creature with large luminous eyes and misty gray fur has been observed
on several different occasions. One witness, named C. W. Cox, said he was
checking his barn at about 9:30 p.m. when he heard a noise in a nearby creek.
Shining his flashlight on the ravine, Cox caught a glimpse of the monster,
which :immediately ran off into the woods. Cox said it definitely walked like a
man, not like a bear. On another occasion, two brothers walking along a coun­
try road in the same district saw the monster rise up in front of them. They
described it as seven or eight feet tall and looking like a gorilla. Sheriff's
deputies were called in on the hunt•••••Last June, a fisherman in St. Clair,
Michigan, hauled up a weird 3.5-pound creature from a deep river and baffled
local marine biologists. The creature has 90 sets of teeth. It has two holes
on the top of its head through which it takes air, which it then passes over

             SAUCER BRIEFSs On the night of September 5th, fifty saucer enthu­
siasts were left stranded at a pier in Waukegan, Illinois, when the skipper of
a boat they had chartered walked out on them. Norman Jack Maynard, leader of

ation, had to refund $1.25 to each of the people, some of whom came from as
far away as 100 miles to take the chartered saucer-hunting excursion. The re­
searchers gathered on the deck of the boat, tied to the pier, and made at­
tempts to signal spaceships with lights; but the skipper, who earlier in the
evening sat skeptically through a two-hour meeting of the group, was nowhere
to be foundl •••Ray Palmer, of science fiction and flying saucer fame, has come

              Since we began publishing SAUCER NEWS in 19.54, many rumors have
been ci.l"aulated concerning your Editor, mainly in regard to his source of in­
come and the fact that he spent long periods of time away from his Fort Lee
Headquarters. Sparking the rumors was the fact that for several years SAUCER
NEWS propounded the theoey that flying saucers are made on Earth, rather than
being from °'1ter Space. This, plus the fact that we make fairly frequent trips
to the Pentagon to gather UFO information, gave rise to the idea that your Ed­
itor is a Government agent of some sort, - in the pay of the Air Force, the
CIA, the "Silence Group." or what-have-you.
              In a non-scheduled Newsletter dated May 10th, 1961, we explained
that business in South America was responsible for your Editor being away so
much. and that the entire mystery concerning our activities would be cleared
up in a forthcoming book. Then, in the June 1962 issue of SAUCER NEWS, we of­
ficially abandoned the "Earth Theory" regarding the origin of saucers, and
stated our present belief - that UFO's are probably extraterrestrial in ori­
gin, but that absolute� of this or any other theoey is still lacking.
              The book never appeared, however, and we never explained to our
readers the nature of our business in South America. Finally, on the morning
of November 8th, 1963, listeners to radio station WOR in New York and to her
sister .50,000 watt station in Boston were treated to a five-hour broadcast
over the Long John Show, in which your F.ditor "told all." The truth is that
from 19.54 to 1961 we were engaged in treasure hunting ventures in Peru, often
with great success. Unfortunately, treasure hunting is illegal in Peru, ex­
cept when performed by qualified archaeologists with a special permit from the
local government. Most of the treasure hunting done in Peru results in the
finding of valuable gold and clay antiques made by the Incas and pre-Incas,
who lived in that land before the Spanish Conquest of 1.530. These antiques
cannot be sold easily in Peru, and must be exported to the United States or
Europe, where there is a ready market among dealers, museums, and wealthy pri­
vate collectors. But Peru, as well as most other countries of the world, has
laws protecting its national treasures, and thus the finding of the antiques
as well as their exportation was illegal. However, n2 United States statutes
were broken, because (strangely enough!) our country has apparently deliber­
ately made its laws in such a way as to encourage the importation of art ob­
jects, no matter hov "cloudy" their origin may be.
              The story is too long to explain in detail here, and in any case
it is certainly outside the scope of a magazine devoted to flying saucers.
Your Editor's adventures in South America make a fascinating tale, however,
and they have been written up in a .5,000 word article for a current issue of
�gosy Magazine. As of this writing, the story has been accepted by �gosy,
but we do not yet know which issue it will appear in. If we find out before
time to mail out•   this issue of SAUCER NEWS, we will write the month in the
space at the bottom of this page. If nothing appears there, watch for the next
couple of issues of �gosy, and you will find our article, which is tentative­
ly titled "I Smuggled a Fortune in Inoa Treasure out of Peru." In reading it,
keep in mind that events have been "telescoped" for literary effect and that
same of the names and locations have been changed to protect former partners.
other than this, everything in the article is true.
              Certain circumstances, explained in the Argosy article, prevent
your Editor fr011 returning to Peru in the near future. Therefore, let us state
in closing that if any serious person with the necessary funds would like to
go there and export several thousand dollars in gold lega]Jy to the United
States, he should get in touch with us. We have information as to the�-

            As I have clearly stated in previous correspondence, it is my firm
conviction that the Air Foree has forfeited the right to be the investigating
body concerning unide ntified nying objects. It is with both sadness and grat­
ification that I present you with further evidence to bolster this conviction&
            On October 31st, 1963, I sent the Air Foree a letter requesting to
be informed of their conclusions concerning two UFO eases. One was the January
20th, 1951 case at Sioux City, Iowa, where a Mid-Continent Airlines crew re­
ported a large UFO. The other ease was the November 5th, 1957 incident at Long
Beach, California, where the Air Force weathermen reporte d six UF0 1 s. On No­
vember 19th I received their reply.
            Their conclusion for the first cas e is as follows, "The January
29th, 1951 sighting at Sioux City, Iowa, carried an initial evaluation of air­
craft by the investigating committee in 1951. However, subsequent re view indi­
cates there may have been possible astronomical as pects to this sighting, in­
volving the planet Jupiter (Magnitude -1.7), which was located at 10 degrees
elevation in the west. The star Deneb was in the northwest as the re porting
aircraft pilot was taking off, and the star Regulus in the east. The setting
of the planet Jupiter corresponds with the final report. However, the case
still retains its original evaluation as aircraft." Not even a vague hint was
made that this case was listed as "unknown" in Project Blue Book Report #14,
released in Octobe r, 1955. One is left to wonder how any astronomical body can
appear as a large, s olid object, relatively close to the observer. One is also
left to puzzle over what type of aircraft has no engine nacelles or jet pods,
and is capable of performing a violent directional change, as this UFO was re­
ported to have done.
            Their conclusion for the Long Beach case••••also typifies the fate
that bas befalle n hundreds of equally inexplicable reports•••••! would like to
mention that when you interviewed Project Blue Book pe rsonnel a couple of
years ago, you seemingly came away greatly impressed that they were really
trying to do an honest and adequate job. The account of this interview in SAU­
CER NEWS (Sept. 1962 issue) clearly showed that you missed a great opportunity
to throw the book at them. Instead, you e ngaged in generalities and genial
conversation, and stayed on very safe ground, and did not e ngage in battle. If
you have another opportunity to personally confront the m, perhaps you will
then demand to know why! why! why! I certainly hope so.
                         HERBERT S. TAYLOR, Brooklyn, New York

            In August of 1963, the Discussion and Lecture Committee of SAUCER
NEWS began its present policy of sponsoring a "name" lecturer every month. We
started the program with Gray Barker on August 16th, and on September 20th we
sponsored Stewart Robb, the noted psychic researcher, who gave a fascinating
talk on the prophecies of Nostradamus. These lectures have already been de­
scribed in detail in our December 1963 issue.
            On October 18th, Gordon Evans was our guest. Mr. Evans is a well­
know political writer and an avid saucer enthusiast. His topic for our group
was "The Case for Mars," and in our opinion this was one of the most interest­
ing factual dissertations we have ever heard. No one who was present that
night could have failed to be impressed by Mr. Evans• wealth of scientific
lmowledge pointing to life on Ma.rs, as well as his amazing familiarity with
all aspects of our space program. Originally, Dr. Leon Davidson (formerly a
frequent contributor to SAUCER NEWS) was scheduled to speak for us on October
18th concerning the alleged role played in saucer research by the Central In­
telligence Agency. Dr. Davidson was forced to cancel, for personal reasons. We
do not mean to be facetious or in any way disrespectful to Gordon Evans when
we wonder out loud if perhaps Dr. Davidson was replaced on our speaking roster
by a representative of the same agency he intended to talk about. In any case,
it was a wonderful and highly informative evening.
            On November 15th our guest was Long John Nebel, of all-night radio
fame. Anticipating a very large turn-out, we abandoned our regular hall at 108
West 56th St. and hired the Palm Room at the Hotel Diplomat on 4Jrd St. That
evening was definitely the high point of our Fall schedule, though unfortu­
nately the attendance was disappointing. Apparently many saucer fans stayed
away thinking that Long John would discuss other topics, whereas the general
public stayed away thinking it would be a saucer lecture. The people who did
come were richly rewarded, however. Long John, who received no payment for his
appearance, gave the audience two hours of fascinating anecdotes and inside
stories about how his 5-hour nightly radio program is run;It took a true show-

            On January 17th our speaker was. John Mittl., a saucer contactee
from the •ountains of Pennsylvania. His topic, called "The Tru.e Nature and

              Book                         Reviews
                            - by Richard E. Wallace -
(Rayiever•s lf2a1 The reader who might be in disagreement with the following
book reviews may question our reasons for writing them. We wish to make it
perfectly clear that each and every volume sumitted to us is care.fully and
impartially studied before any opinions are formulated. If we find a book
worthy of praise, it shall be praised. If we find a book worthy of criticism,
it shall be criticized. It is the privilege of the reader to hold whatever
view he chooses.)
THE STRANGE CASE OF DR, M, K, JESSUP - edited by Gray Barker (Saucerian Books)
                "At 6130 P••• on April 20th, 1959, the body of Dr. M. K. Jessup,
  astrancaer, researcher, and writer on the subject of 1 fiying saucers, 1 was
  .t'ound in his station wagon in a park near Coral Gables, F1orida.
                "Medical examiners labeled the death •suicide.••
                In this manner the notorious Gray Barker begins the Introduction
  of his latest and worst book, "The Strange Case of Dr. M. K. Jessup." In pre-
, vious issues, the Editor of this magazine has severely criticized Mr. Barker
  on a wide Tariety of topics, and not without justif'ication. Not being person­
  ally acquainted with Barker, we perhaps should have had no predisposition to
  be anything but complimentary in this essay. However, in recent years Mr. Bar­
  ker has attained a widespread reputation for his mercenary activities which do
  not natter him in the least.
                Dr. Jessup 1 s tragic and untimely death was indeed a shock to .his
  many readers and colleagues, and it is regrettable that Barker and his cohorts
  have had to ressurect an issue that would be better left to unpleasant recol­
  lection rather than used as another vehicle tor profitable exploitation.
                For reasons of decency, we shall not delve too deeply into the
contents of this miserable volume. Suffice is to say that Mr. Barker employs
his now familiar cloak-and-dagger tactics to the extreme in his presentation
of the carelessly-edited sentiments of weird and obviously non-existent char­
acters who go by the names of "Carlos Miguel Allende" and "Colonel B." The
contents of this book are in unusually bad taste and were undoubtedly designed
for the sole purpose of reaping a ripe financial harvest for the publisher.
llii � .Ql FLYING SAUCERS .. by Donald H. Menzel and Lyle G. Boyd (Doubleday)
            When this tome was submitted to us for review, shrieks of laughter
emanating from Officialdom became almost audible. We must admit that it was
with great trepidation that we proceeded to peruse this book. It is W our
policy to tackle the task of commentary with any preconceptions. However, in
the case of Menzel, this is utterly impossible!
            Having had the dubious honor of reading his first written work on
the topic of UF0 1 s, and considering it to be in its own way vastly inferior to
many of the contactee books, it did not require any extrasensory abilities to
perceive the manner in which Menzel would subtly excoriate the scientific
method to further his dogmatism.
            It is quite obvious that Menzel and his co-author entered into
this project with the preconceived notion that flying saucers cannot be a DlBJl­
ifestation of reality, and that every shred of evidence proving otherwise may
be safely discarded without further ado. Even the Air Force, with infinitely
superior facilities available, has been incapable of inventing solutions for
every UFO sighting. Nevertheless, Menzel, through some quirk of fate, appears
to have somehow acquired previously unsuspected powers of intellect to achieve
his desire to destroy the saucer enigma once and for all.
            Had we sufficient space, we might tackle a detailed debunking of
the varied and intricate fallacious explanations that Menzel ascribes to DlBJlY
saucer sightings. However, we are confident that the reader will have little
difficulty in this endeavor!
            All books that• appear in print are published with some purpose in
mind. In the vast• majority of instances, this might revolve around financial
benefit. In the remainder of cases, the author wishes to expound certain
•truths" that he feels must reach an audience. In view or Doubleday's half­
hearted promotion of Menzel's volume, however, its purpose in being was at
             •
first quite obscure   to us, and was clarified only upon a careful exam1nation
of the motivations of the authors.
            The question arises as to why Menzel should have considered writ­
ing a book that would quite obviously produce minimal financial gain or pres­
tige for him. Perhaps the answer lies in his fanatical denial of UFO reality.
During recent years, one gets the distinct impression that Menzel was slowly
being converted from staunch skepticism about UFO 1 s to a partial acceptance of
their possible actuality. Menzel was unable to permit himself to become con­
vinced of the UFO 1 s existence, so he was compelled to vrite a bGok that would
not only fortify the disbelief of others, but of himself as well!
            Mrs. Boyd's intentions are even more fascinating. She purports to
be a science-fiction writer, though we shall hasten to concede that we have
not bad the misfortune of reading any of her work. It may interest the reader
to know that in the February, 1964 issue of "Worlds of Tomorrow," a science­
fiction publication that carries factual material as a regular feature, there
appears an article by Mrs. Boyd that alludes to the possibility that Jonathan
Swift might have been a visitor from the planet Mars. Yet, in "The World of
Flying Saucers," she is apparently quite unwilling to accept even the possi­
bility of UFO reality, let alone the remote possibility that the famous author
of "Gulliver's Travels" might have come to earth from another world. We cannot
but suspect her motives in writing a negative saucer book.
10.
            If we might be penuitted an opportunity to make a humble predic­
tion, we would say that this volume will almost surely be a pitiful failure;
and we are confident that the reader will assist to this end by not purchasing
the book.

INSIDE m SAUCERS � -           "edited and annotated" by Timothy Green Beckley
            In view of the number of grandiose and extravagant claims made by
the author of this book, one would be led to believe that it would disclose,
after a decade and a half of fruitless research, the final and unequivocal
solution to the saucer mystery.
            As we breathlessly paged through the tome, we anxiously awaited
the fantastic revelations promised us by Mr. Beckleys The actual nature of the
Three Men in Black; the reason the Air Force sends up mysterious satellites in
polar orbits; 11 earthshaking" information concerning a "past researcher" (??);
why greater quantities of 11 angel hair 11 have fallen from the skies during re­
cent years; the startling reasons behind Air Force secrecy; the identity of
the enigmatic agency allegedly in real charge of UFO information; and so forth.
            Surely if but one of these outrageous claims were true, Mr. Beck­
ley's work would rank among the most significant and influential of saucer
literature. But "Inside the Saucers 196211 falls far short of fulfilling even
the most inconsequential of its outlandish claims. One day someone may be able
to reveal to us mortals these startling disclosures. However, in all probabil­
ity this "someone" will not bear the name of Timothy Green Beckley.
            The book is offered as a professionally printed volume. Thus, we
were somewhat dismayed to discover that it is poorly mimeographed to the ex­
tent that it was often a chore to decipher. This inadequacy might be excused
if the writing were of a readable nature. The young Mr. Beckley's lack of pro­
ficiency with the written word, however, was never more in evidence. His style
is not only tedious, awkward, and occasionally incoherent, but is often
plagued by grammatical errors.
            The one redeeming feature in the entire book is a fairly interest­
ing account of the UFO sightings of 1962, but this section is often marred by
insufficient and ambigious information. Our reco!IDllendation is that Mr. Beckley
pay more attention to his studies during English class in High School, and re­
frain from further book publishing enterprises until he acquires more knowl­
edge of his native tongue.
